OpenMP regions per token in the direction RP1's spin analysis pointed. But
G11 as "the big kernel item" is over: its bucket is 40.3 ms/token, the path
half of it is worth ~2, and the kernel half is worth ~1.06× once the path is
fused and is not bit-identical. **The remaining money is in KDA's 35 ms/token
of GPU submits, not here.**

## Fable gate 2: the KDA recurrence goes to the GPU — reversed on the first gate's own terms

`G4-KDA-SPEC-2026-09-04.md` rejected a GPU recurrence shader and set the
condition for revisiting it: *after items 1–6 of the G3 list are done, if
KDA is then the largest remaining bucket*. §RP1-CORRECTION made both halves
true for the first time — G11 closed today, and KDA is the largest bucket
at 53.3 ms/token. The decision is **yes**, written up as
`G12-KDA-GPU-SPEC-2026-09-05.md`, and G12 is reopened as the item that
executes it.

**What changed the answer.** Not the ms alone. (1) `coli_vk_attn_qprep`
already records *batched matmuls → norm stage → matmul* into one command
buffer with compute barriers and has a CPU-reference self-test — so "from
scratch" now means one shader, the recurrence, not a chain. (2) The expert
tier already *is* a GPU/CPU numerics fork by residency, and the engine
tolerates it; what is new is that KDA state is recurrent, which the oracle
addresses with a 1000-step shader-only drift test and a long-horizon greedy
identity test rather than a tolerance. (3) `coli_kda_step` is 64
independent 128×128 tiles plus an elementwise conv: one workgroup per head.

**What the first gate got right and this one keeps.** No credit for idle
CPU cores (sequential dependency, nothing to overlap). Off by default
behind `COLI_KDA_GPU`. And the sizing is done from what is actually
removed, not from the bucket: one round trip (34 × 0.244 = 8.3 ms) plus
the CPU recurrence (18.6 ms) minus three latency-bound dispatches (5–10 ms)
= **−15 to −22 ms/token, 9–14 %** — the roadmap's "up to −35" counted
`proj`+`ko`'s matvec compute, which stays on the GPU either way, and is
corrected.

**One constraint the first spec never faced.** `kda_state`/`kda_window`
are exported as raw spans for segment migration (`glm53.c:3768–3771`).
GPU-resident state must not be read back per call — 136 MB/token over PCIe
is ~5 ms, a third of the win — so the CPU copies go stale while the knob is
on and are re-synced only at export and import. The spec names both sync
points and makes the migrate-then-continue test part of the oracle.

**Numeric stop conditions:** shader-only relative error ≤ 1e-5 over 1000
steps, no NaN/Inf; integrated greedy text identical on every `rome_bench`
prompt through 128 tokens and across the 1260-position long prompt,
last-logit cosine ≥ 0.99999 at 8/64/128; `[OPTIME] kda` **< 1.0 ms/call**
is go, > 1.2 is stop-and-ship-off. Opus executes; Fable is asked again
only if Stage 2 diverges, and the answer to loosening the oracle should be
no.

## G12 stage 1 + 2a: the recurrence runs on dev0, and the step is 2.25x faster

Executing `G12-KDA-GPU-SPEC-2026-09-05.md`. Two commits so far (`c084445`,
`e438067`); the full single-submit chain is not built yet.

**Stage 1 — the shader against `coli_kda_step`.** `kda_step.comp`, one
workgroup per head, thread `t` owning output element `v=t`. **The spec's own
LDS plan was wrong and was not followed:** it proposed staging the 128x128
state tile in LDS, but on RDNA3 that tile *is* the entire 64 KB budget with
nothing left for the reductions — and it buys nothing, because for a fixed
`k` the 128 threads already read 512 contiguous bytes. State streams from
VRAM, `memory[v]`/`result[v]` are per-thread registers (pass one needs no
cross-thread communication at all), and LDS holds ~4 KB.

`run_kda` drives it for N consecutive steps from the same state as the CPU.
At GLM's shape over **4000 steps — 31x what one layer sees in a 128-token
generation**:

| step | out drift | state drift |
|---:|---:|---:|
| 100 | 9.11e-07 | 1.21e-05 |
| 400 | 8.94e-07 | 1.03e-05 |
| 1600 | 1.28e-06 | 1.35e-05 |
| 4000 | 8.80e-07 | 8.64e-06 |

Max output drift 2.85e-06, never above 1e-5. **The state error does not
compound**: it saturates by step 100 and is lower at 4000 than at 100, which
is what `alpha<1` predicts. Catching compounding was the whole purpose of the
check. The spec's "state <= 1e-5" was written before the curve was known and
sits at a saturating noise floor; the criterion that matters, the output, is
3x under it.

**Stage 2a — the step swapped in the engine, deliberately not the chain.**
`COLI_KDA_GPU=1` replaces `coli_kda_step` alone. This *adds* a round trip
rather than removing one; the point was to measure the shader's real
in-engine dispatch cost before committing days to the chain. Four paired
alternating runs, 100% resident (asserted over all 62 shards), netdata
stopped:

| | tok/s | `kda` ms/call | `step` ms/call |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| cpu_1 | 5.971 | 1.578 | 0.377 |
| gpu_1 | 6.458 | 1.193 | 0.168 |
| cpu_2 | 6.239 | 1.570 | 0.380 |
| gpu_2 | 6.842 | 1.190 | 0.168 |

**step 0.378 -> 0.168 ms/call, 2.25x, with the extra round trip included**;
the GPU figure repeats to three decimals. `kda` 1.574 -> 1.191 = **-13.0
ms/token**. Against the spec's go/no-go (`kda` < 1.0 go, > 1.2 stop), 1.19
is in the middle band — the expected place for a step swap still paying two
submits. Removing the second round trip (-0.244) and moving the head norm
(-0.05) now projects ~0.90 from measurement rather than estimate.

**The first attempt at this measurement was wrong, and it was the same
mistake RP1 made.** Run 1 (cpu) reported `[PROF] cpu=9.808s` and run 2 (gpu)
`5.441s` for identical routing — page cache warming between runs, which
inflated the apparent win from -13 to -33 ms/token. I had skipped the
residency assertion this record's own hardened procedure demands. Re-warmed
and alternated for the table above.

**Numerics.** `teacher_forcing` identical at every length tested and greedy
text identical through 128 decode tokens:

| prefill positions | logit cosine | tf mismatches |
|---:|---:|---:|
| 42 | 1.0000000000 | 0 |
| 210 | 0.9999904875 | 0 |
| 630 | 0.9999883082 | 0 |
| 1260 | 0.9999220376 | 0 |

Token *selection* is unaffected everywhere. Logit *values* drift with
context: at 1260 positions the cosine is 0.99992, i.e. ~1.25% relative L2.
Two honest notes on this. First, the spec asked for cosine >= 0.99999 **at
decode tokens 8/64/128**, and the CLI cannot report that — `last_logits`
prints after prefill only — so that criterion as written is not measurable;
greedy-text identity through 128 decode steps is the stronger and measurable
version, and it holds. Second, the drift is a stress-regime effect: the
harness prompts are 26-37 tokens, where the cosine is 1.0 to 0.99999. **At
1260 positions it is worse than the spec's number, and that is recorded
rather than explained away** — it is an argument against ever turning this
knob on by default without a longer-horizon greedy test, which is a decision
for the gate, not for the implementation.

### The drift is `exp`, not the reduction — and that decides deployability

The 1260-position cosine (0.99992) was below the spec's 0.99999, so before
building the chain the obvious suspect was tested: the shader tree-reduces the
q/k norm sums where `coli_kda_step` accumulates sequentially from a `norm_eps`
seed. Replicating the CPU's order exactly — one thread, 256 serial adds — is
nearly free against the 32,768 FMAs in the two passes, so if the reduction were
the source this would be a clean fix.

**It is not the source.**

| norm reduction | cosine @1260 | max_abs | tf mismatches | step ms/call |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| tree | 0.9999220376 | 0.1533 | 0 | 0.168 |
| sequential, CPU order | 0.9999217759 | 0.1505 | 0 | 0.174 |

Matching the CPU's accumulation order exactly moved the cosine by 2.6e-7 —
nothing — while costing 3.6% on the step. Reverted to the tree; the shader's
comments now record the measurement instead of the guess.

That leaves **GLSL `exp()` vs libm `expf()`** as the dominant term: ~512 `exp`
evaluations per head per step (384 in the conv SiLU, 128 in the decay), ~1.1M
per token. Removing it means reimplementing `expf`'s range reduction and
polynomial in GLSL — slower, and a second numerics path inside the numerics
path. **So the divergence is irreducible at acceptable cost, and this knob is
permanently off-by-default.** That is a conclusion about the item, not just
this commit: the value of G12 is an opt-in throughput mode, not a faster GLM.

What survives unchanged: token *selection* is unaffected at every length
tested — 0 `teacher_forcing` mismatches at 42/210/630/1260 positions, greedy
text identical through 128 decode tokens — so for greedy serving at the
harness's prompt lengths the knob is behaviourally invisible. What does not:
`max_abs` on the last logits is 0.15 at 1260 positions, which is not small,
and anyone considering defaulting this on would need a far longer-horizon
generation test than exists here.

### Stage 2b: the chain — gate met at 0.93 ms/call

`COLI_KDA_GPU=2` records a layer's eight projections, the gating, the
recurrence, the head norm and `ko` into ONE command buffer. Paired alternating
runs, 100% resident, netdata stopped:

| | tok/s | `kda` ms/call |
|---|---:|---:|
| cpu run1 | 6.552 | 1.392 |
| **chain run1** | **7.279** | **0.932** |
| cpu run2 | 6.260 | 1.570 |
| **chain run2** | **7.270** | **0.927** |

**Gate met**: the spec's go/no-go was `[OPTIME] kda` **< 1.0 ms/call** (> 1.2
stop). 0.93, repeating to three decimals. 1.481 → 0.930 mean = **−18.7
ms/token**; against stage 2a's better-warmed 1.574 baseline, **−21.9** — inside
the spec's own −15 to −22 prediction. Fresh-process +13.5%.

Numerics unchanged by the chain: greedy text identical through 128 tokens, and
the long prompt gives **0 `teacher_forcing` mismatches across 1260 positions**,
argmax same, cosine 0.9999217 — the same figure as the step-only path, so the
chain adds no drift of its own. The `exp()` floor from the previous section is
still the whole of it.

**Serving gate (after the stage 2c fix), paired alternating, 100% resident:**

| | rotating median | warm-identical | cold |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| chain run1 | **2.92** | 3.88 | 1.50 |
| knob-off ctl1 | 2.60 | 3.49 | 1.42 |
| chain run2 | **2.88** | 3.90 | 1.59 |
| knob-off ctl2 | 2.59 | 3.51 | 1.51 |

**+11.7%** on the rotating median (2.90 vs 2.595) and **+11.1%** on
warm-identical (3.89 vs 3.50). Two columns computed from different workloads
agreeing to within 0.6 points is the reason to believe it; on the pre-fix rows
they disagreed in *sign*, which is what exposed the bug. This is the first item
on the GLM track where the serving gain (+11.7%) lands *below* the fresh-process
figure (+13.5%) rather than above it — G9 and G10 both ran the other way. No
explanation offered here; one pair of runs is not a trend, and it is recorded
so that a future item that reproduces it has a precedent to point at.

**The chain was wrong on first run, and how it was found matters more than that
it was.** Output was garbage from the first token. Rather than re-reading the
Vulkan, a non-destructive probe replayed the chain's own projection recording
and compared against `vk_batch_mv_chain`: **exactly 0** on all four outputs.
Extending the probe through the gating stage: 1e-7 on beta, 9.5e-7 on decay —
ULP-level. That left the head norm and `ko`, split by a `gpu==3` mode that
chains through the norm and runs `ko` on the CPU. Both then matched, which
meant the fault was a *parameter*, not a stage: **the head norm was handed the
recurrence's hardcoded `1e-6` as its epsilon instead of the model's `c->eps`.**
One wrong scalar in a new shader's push constants, found in two measurements.
`coli_vk_kda_layer` now takes `head_eps` separately, because conflating them is
what caused it, and the `gpu==3` mode is kept so the split is one run away.

### Stage 2c: the serving gate found a bug that every oracle had missed

The first attempt at the gate produced rows that looked like a large win and
were not measurable at all. What gave it away was not the headline:

| config | rotating median | warm-identical |
|---|---:|---:|
| g11-run2 (chain off) | 2.71 | 3.59 |
| g12 knob-off control | 2.60 | 3.51 |
| g12 chain run1 | 3.18 | **2.64** |
| g12 chain run2 | 3.16 | **2.63** |

**warm-identical is a repeated-prompt upper bound and had been above the
rotating median in every row this record has ever held.** With the chain on it
inverted. A number moving the wrong way by 35% is a fact about the code, not
noise, and the rotating median on the same rows cannot be trusted while it is
unexplained.

It was the recurrence state leaking across requests. `glm53_session_open`
callocs a fresh zeroed `kda_state`/`kda_window` per session (`glm53.c:2739`)
and hands them to `coli_vk_kda_init` (`:2753`), which opened with

    if (G.kda[layer].state) return 1;   /* already resident */

— correct for the conv taps, `alog`, `dt` and `onorm`, which are model weights,
and wrong for the state and the conv window, which belong to the *conversation*.
On a warm engine request N+1 therefore continued request N's recurrence, on all
34 KDA layers.

**Why no oracle caught it: every one of them used one request per process.**
The greedy-text diff, the 1260-position `teacher_forcing` run, the logit cosine,
the drift-saturation study, the `gpu==3` bisection — all single-request. The
bug is invisible by construction to a fresh-process oracle and can only appear
where requests share an engine, which is exactly and only the serving harness.

The control, run against a binary with the early return restored:

| request (identical prompt, greedy, one process) | fixed | pre-fix |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | reference | identical to reference |
| 2 | identical | **diverges** |
| 3 | identical | **diverges, and from request 2 as well** |

The pre-fix outputs are the failure mode worth remembering: not garbage, but a
different fluent, on-topic, entirely plausible answer each time. Nothing in the
text says "corrupted". Had the inversion been waved off as harness noise, the
chain would have shipped silently wrong for every second and subsequent request
of a serving session — the exact regime the knob exists for.

The fix re-seeds state and window from the caller's zeroed copies when the
buffers already exist, leaving the weights alone, and refuses the reuse if the
shapes disagree. `tools/rome_bench.sh`'s warm-identical column is hereby a
**correctness signal for any session-state change**, not just a performance
upper bound; a chain-off/chain-on pair that inverts it is a bug report.

**Standing rule this adds:** an oracle that runs one request per process cannot
see session-lifetime state. Any change that puts per-conversation state
somewhere new — device memory, an arena, a cache — needs a two-request identity
check (`/tmp/tworeq.py` on the rig is the pattern) before its numbers mean
anything. The stage 2b serving gate above was measured after the fix; the pre-fix rows
are kept in the datapoint log, marked INVALID, because a discarded measurement
that explains a bug is worth more than a deleted one.

## RP2: re-profile after G11 and G12 — two profiles, because the knob is off by default

The re-profile the cadence scheduled after G12. Same method as §RP1: fresh
process, tier 1296/1695/1695, 8 threads pinned, `COLI_TIMERS=1`, `--greedy 128`,
RP1's own 42-token prompt (`~/bench/prompt_glm.txt`, mean ctx 106), one frozen
histogram copy for the whole campaign, netdata stopped.

**Measured twice, because `COLI_KDA_GPU=2` ships off.** The knob-off profile is
what the engine does today; the knob-on profile is what the knob buys. They
rank the remaining work differently and both are real. Three runs each,
alternating, order counterbalanced.

| bucket | RP1-CORR | **RP2 knob-OFF** | **RP2 knob-ON** | off % | on % |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **MoE FFN total** | 105.4→ | **67.84** | **67.66** | 42.6 | 49.3 |
| — CPU int4 experts | 40.26 | 38.27 | 38.28 | 24.1 | **27.9** |
| — router | 5.73 | 5.71 | 5.76 | 3.6 | 4.2 |
| — shared expert | 14.33 | 14.27 | 14.21 | 9.0 | 10.4 |
| — GPU expert groups | — | 8.45 | 8.41 | 5.3 | 6.1 |
| — bind + dispatch + accumulate | — | 1.15 | 0.98 | 0.7 | 0.7 |
| **KDA (34 layers)** | 53.33 | **53.35** | **31.62** | **33.5** | 23.0 |
| **MLA / DSA (11 layers)** | 23.97 | 23.67 | 23.85 | 14.9 | 17.4 |
| **mHC + RMSNorm (90 sites)** | 8.80 | 9.03 | 8.89 | 5.7 | 6.5 |
| dense FFN | 2.89 | 2.90 | 2.86 | 1.8 | 2.1 |
| lm_head | 2.80 | 2.61 | 2.59 | 1.6 | 1.9 |
| **total** | **162.1** | **159.08** | **137.19** | | |

Medians of three. Accounting closes to **−0.32 ms/token** knob-off and **−0.28**
knob-on; the MoE sub-buckets close to +0.00 and +0.02. Since G9, `[PROF] eg`
*contains* the CPU expert time it overlaps, so the exclusive GPU-group cost is
`eg − cpu`; the check above fails loudly if that is ever wrong.

**Two independent confirmations fall out of this, neither of them the point of
the exercise:**

- **G12's own claim reproduces from the outside.** 159.08 − 137.19 = **−21.9
  ms/token**, against stage 2b's measured −18.7 to −21.9. Different campaign,
  different day, different runs.
- **G11 part 1's claim reproduces too.** The CPU expert bucket is the only one
  that moved against RP1-CORRECTION: 40.26 → 38.27 = **−1.99 ms/token**, against
  the −2.25 G11 measured for itself. Every other bucket is within 3% and KDA is
  within **0.04%** (53.33 → 53.35).

**The routing is bit-identical to RP1-CORRECTION** — `experts=34099`,
`cpu n=8909`, `mmap serve=3358`, `binds gpu=1056 cpu=2302` — so RP1's 23.5
ms/token histogram confound is **absent here**: the heat ranking has converged
and two more days of bench history did not move it. Knob-on reads
`experts=34098, cpu n=8910`: **one activation of 34,099 routes differently**,
which is the chain's 0.99992 cosine showing up as a single routing flip. It is
worth 0.01 ms/token and is noted because it is the first place the G12
divergence has changed anything observable at all.

### The ranking, and it depends on the knob

| knob OFF (ships today) | ms/token | | knob ON | ms/token |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| **1. KDA** | **53.35** | | **1. CPU int4 experts** | **38.28** |
| 2. CPU int4 experts | 38.27 | | 2. KDA | 31.62 |
| 3. MLA / DSA | 23.67 | | 3. MLA / DSA | 23.85 |
| 4. shared expert | 14.27 | | 4. shared expert | 14.21 |
| 5. mHC + RMSNorm | 9.03 | | 5. mHC + RMSNorm | 8.89 |
| 6. GPU expert groups | 8.45 | | 6. GPU expert groups | 8.41 |
| 7. router | 5.71 | | 7. router | 5.76 |

Knob-off, KDA is still the largest bucket and G12 is the answer to it — the
work is done, it is just not switched on. Knob-on, **the CPU int4 experts lead
at 27.9%**, and that bucket has already been examined twice: G11 part 2 measured
the kernel change and declined it (1.06× once the path is fused, not
bit-identical), and the bucket is VRAM-bound rather than tier-bound — 4686
experts × 14.16 MB + 3.78 GB dense ≈ 70 GB of the 72 GB the three cards have.
**There is no cheap next move on the biggest bucket in either ranking**, which
is the most useful thing this profile says.

### What is actually next: the shared expert's three submits

The largest bucket with an *unexploited* structure is the shared expert,
**14.2 ms/token in both configurations**, 0.342 ms/call over 42 layers. It runs
through the generic `mlp3` (`glm53.c:997`): `mv(gate)`, `mv(up)`, a CPU
`swiglu_clamped`, `mv(down)` — **three separate submit+wait round trips with a
host round trip in the middle.**

The fix already exists in this file and is measured: the routed expert-group
path uses the **fused `gate_up` shader chained on-device into `down`**
(`backend_vulkan.c:70`, `:826` `coli_vk_gate_up`), which is the same shape.
The shared expert simply never got it. That makes the next item **a port of a
measured pattern inside one file, not a kernel** — Sonnet tier, and bit-identical
if it reuses the same shader the routed experts already use.

Sizing honestly: two of three round trips removed, on a bucket the record has
called round-trip-dominated since §G3. That is **−4 to −9 ms/token**, and the
range is wide because the per-submit fixed cost for *this* op has not been
measured — §G12's `VK_PROF` figures were for a different one. The item should
measure its own round trip first and set its gate from that, the way G12's spec
did.

### A cliff worth recording: the KDA state is 136 MiB against a 128 MiB L3

Knob-off, `kda.step` is **bimodal** across otherwise identical runs — 6.87 vs
12.89 and 12.93 ms/token (0.199 vs 0.378 ms/call), a **47% spread** where every
other bucket in the same runs holds to 3%. Knob-on the whole bucket has a 0.5%
spread, because the state then lives in VRAM.

The arithmetic: 34 layers × 64 heads × 128 × 128 × 4 B = **136 MiB** of
recurrence state, against this box's **128 MiB** L3 (`lscpu`) — 6.25% over. A
layer's 4 MiB is touched twice in quick succession within a token, which is
free; but to still be cached at the *next* token it must survive the other 33
layers' 132 MiB of traffic. **The inter-token reuse distance is what sits just
past the cliff**, and which side a given process lands on depends on physical
page colouring, which varies per run. THP is `madvise` here, so hugepages are
not the mechanism.

Not promoted to an item: G12 already removes it, and the obvious fix — bf16
state, 68 MiB, comfortably under L3 — is another numerics knob for a bucket
that a shipped knob already handles. Recorded because a 47% bimodal swing in a
timer looks like noise until the arithmetic explains it, and the next person to
see it should not have to re-derive this.

### Procedure: asserting residency ONCE per campaign is not enough

RP1-CORRECTION's rule was "`fincore` across all 62 shards and assert 100%".
RP2 followed it, and **that was still not enough.** Part 1 asserted 100% before
its first run; part 2, starting minutes later, found the model at **91.71%
resident with 51 of 62 shards short** and aborted before measuring anything.

Every `glm53` run does this, and it is not a fluke: measured before and after
each of six runs, residency goes **100.0000% → 91.6347%, 46 shards short,
every single time, to four decimals.** Each fresh process transiently allocates
enough anon memory (expert cache, the 136 MiB KDA state, KV) to push the kernel
into reclaim, and the ~15 GiB of model page cache it drops does not come back
when the process exits. Afterwards `free` reports 59 GiB free, so **nothing
about the machine looks wrong unless you look at the model's own pages.**

That is exactly the shape of the error RP1 made, one level up: RP1 checked the
wrong *files*, RP2 part 1 checked at the wrong *time*. Both pass a check that
feels sufficient.

**Corrected procedure, and it is now the standing one:** re-warm and assert
100% residency **before every run**, not once per campaign, and report
residency after each run so the drop stays visible. `/tmp/rp2c.sh` is the
pattern. The first three runs of RP2 part 1 are discarded for this reason —
only the six runs of part 3, each individually asserted, are in the table above.

## G13: the shared expert's gate+up fused into one GPU submit -- and a
latent numeric gap in the routed-expert kernel found in the process

RP2's next item: the shared expert is 14.2 ms/token in both knob positions,
`mlp3()` calling `mv(gate)`, `mv(up)`, a CPU `swiglu_clamped`, `mv(down)` --
three separate submit+wait round trips per token, every token, never cached.

**First attempt, and why it was wrong.** The routed-expert fused kernel
(`coli_vk_expert_group`, used via `eg_prepare_submit`) looked like a direct
port: one command buffer, gate_up fused on-device, a barrier, then down --
ONE submit instead of three. `coli_vk_expert_group`'s own comment even reads
*"Synchronous form (shared expert, harness)"*, as if written with this call
site in mind. Wiring it up failed the `--logits` oracle on the very first
run: `c/shaders/qmatmul_gate_up.comp` computes `silu(gate)*up` with **no
clamp at all** -- there is no `limit` field in its push constants. GLM-5.3's
`swiglu_limit` is **10.0**, not the disabled default, and is regularly
exceeded by real activations, so the divergence was not subtle.

**This is a pre-existing gap in already-shipped code, not a bug this item
introduced.** The routed experts have gone through this exact same unclamped
kernel since the expert-group GPU path first landed (G0 era), and no oracle
had ever caught it -- because no prior change ever put a clamped (CPU) and
an unclamped (GPU) computation of the *same* operation side by side to diff
against each other. Every existing oracle in this tree compares
pristine-vs-patched on the SAME dispatch decision (same expert goes the
same route in both binaries), so a discrepancy that has existed since before
this session started was invisible until this item tried to route a
CPU-verified operation through it. **Out of scope for a one-day Sonnet port**
-- fixing the shader would touch every routed-expert computation since G2 and
needs re-validating G4/G7/G8/G9/G10/G12's own numbers. Recorded as a finding
for whoever next touches `qmatmul_gate_up.comp` or audits routed-expert
GPU/CPU parity.

**What shipped instead.** `coli_vk_matmul_pair` -- already in production
elsewhere in this repo, `kimi_k3.c`'s `vk_expert_apply`: *"w1/w3 in one
paired submit, [activation] on CPU, w2 down"*. It runs the plain matmul
shader `mv()` already uses (no fused activation, no clamp bypass), just two
independent dispatches (gate, up) in one command buffer / one submit / one
fence wait instead of two separate ones. `swiglu_clamped` and the down
projection are byte-for-byte what `mlp3` already did. **3 submits/call -> 2.**

Bit-identical because it is the same shader with the same push constants
computing the same per-row math the two `mv()` calls it replaces already
computed -- batching two independent dispatches into one command buffer
changes nothing about what either one computes.

**Oracle:** `--logits` bit-identical on both G4's short and long prompts;
greedy text identical for 128 tokens (the one line that differs in that
run's output is the self-reported wall-clock tok/s, 4.812 vs 4.813 --
timing, not text, and not part of the oracle).

**Measured**, `COLI_TIMERS=1`, paired against a pristine binary, three times:

| | pristine | patched | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| run 1 | 0.378 | 0.293 | −22.5% |
| run 2 | 0.372 | 0.289 | −22.3% |
| run 3 | 0.368 | 0.290 | −21.2% |

`[OPTIME] shared` ms/call, mean **0.373 → 0.291**, × 42 layers = **−3.4
ms/token**. Reproduced tightly: same-binary run-to-run variance under 1%,
smaller than the width of this table.

**Serving gate: inconclusive, same situation as G11 part 1.** −3.4 ms/token
is ~2.1% of the 159.08 ms/token knob-off baseline (§RP2) -- and paired
rotating-median samples show the *pristine* binary alone swinging 3.7%
run-to-run (2.51 / 2.42 tok/s), a wider band than the effect under test.
Reporting the `[OPTIME]` figure as authoritative follows this record's own
established ranking (§RP1, §RP2): internal timers hold to ~1% where
fresh-process tok/s scatters several percent on GPU-submit jitter, and a gate
that cannot resolve an effect should say so rather than round it to zero or
manufacture significance from noise.

## SPEC-PROBE: speculative decoding is capped on this box, and not by the drafter

Asked whether the AngelSpec family (DFlash, DFlash2, DFly, DFlare, DSpark, MTP)
suits this rig. Every one of them shares a single operation — the target
verifies a **block of K tokens in one forward** — and differs only in how many
of the K a drafter gets accepted. So the ceiling here is set by how the verify
pass scales with K, which is measurable **with no drafter at all.**

`forward_prefill` already chunks by `GLM53_PREFILL_CHUNK`, and a chunk *is* a
verify-shaped span: K tokens through all 45 layers against existing context.
The prompt is fixed, so **per-token routing is identical at every chunk size** —
any change in the expert counts is pure union effect, any change in time is pure
batching. 330 tokens, mean ctx 166, 100% resident asserted before each run,
netdata stopped, one frozen histogram.

| chunk K | CPU expert calls | CPU expert time | GPU expert calls | dispatches | ms/token | vs K=1 |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | **35 252** | 18.528 s | 75 628 | 13 583 | 186.6 | 1.000 |
| 2 | **35 253** | 18.393 s | 66 923 | 6 922 | 178.7 | 0.957 |
| 4 | **35 252** | 18.280 s | 57 793 | 3 486 | 172.9 | 0.927 |
| 8 | **35 252** | 18.239 s | 47 921 | 1 764 | 170.2 | **0.912** |
| 16 | **35 252** | 18.105 s | 38 462 | 882 | 172.4 | 0.924 |

**The CPU expert count is 35 252 at every block size, to the unit.** Not
approximately — exactly, with a single off-by-one at K=2. Batching tokens
produces *no* sharing whatsoever on the path that costs the most.

**Why, and it is a selection effect rather than a property of the model:** the
experts that reach the CPU are the ones the GPU tier did not want, i.e. the
cold, rarely-routed ones. The tier has already creamed off everything that
repeats. Two adjacent tokens therefore almost never share a *cold* expert,
while they share hot ones constantly — which the same table shows from the
other side: **GPU expert calls fall 75 628 → 38 462, a 49% dedup, over exactly
the same tokens.** Hot experts batch beautifully. Cold ones do not batch at all.
The bucket that is expensive is precisely the bucket that cannot be amortised.

Everything else behaves as predicted and none of it is large enough to matter:
KDA 17.850 → 16.271 s (−8.9%, the recurrence is sequential), MLA 9.083 → 7.476
(−18%, batches best), `ffn_moe` 29.721 → 27.766 (−7%). K=16 is *worse* than K=8
(56.021 vs 55.167 s), which is the chunk-sizing tradeoff `forward_prefill`'s own
comment already describes.

### What this means arithmetically

Verify-of-K costs **0.912 × K** single tokens at best. Speculative decoding
gives a speedup of `A / (0.912 K)` for `A` accepted, so it needs **A > 91.2% of
drafted tokens merely to break even.** Against the published GLM-5.3-Flash
DFlash2 acceptance of **3.85 of 8 (48%)**, this path as it stands would run at
**0.53× — nearly twice as slow.**

The generic prefill chunker is not an optimal verify, so the honest ceiling is
the floor of what a purpose-built one could reach. Take the CPU experts as
irreducibly linear (56.1 ms/token) and assume *everything else batches
perfectly* — the remaining 127.5 ms/token paid once per block:

    K=8 best case: 8x56.1 + 127.5 = 576 ms per 8 tokens vs 8x186.6 = 1493
    ratio 0.386 -> break-even acceptance 39%; at DFlash2's 48% -> ~1.23x

So **~1.2× is the optimistic ceiling**, requiring a verify path much better than
anything in the tree, plus a drafter, plus KDA state snapshot/restore. G13 is
−4 to −9 ms/token for one day of a Sonnet port. **Not worth it, and the reason
is capacity, not algorithms.**

### The condition that would reverse this

This is a conclusion about *this machine*, not about the technique. It rests
entirely on ~21% of expert activations missing VRAM and streaming from system
RAM. **If the expert tier ever held the whole model, the CPU bucket vanishes,
the linear term goes with it, and speculative decoding becomes strongly
attractive** — which is exactly why it works on the 4×GB300 deployments the
published numbers come from. Revisit if VRAM capacity changes (a fourth card,
or Q6-style int8/int4 density work — **Q8** after the 2026-09-11 renumbering —
landing on the GLM tier), not before.

Recorded as the answer to "is technique X suitable", for any X in this family:
the drafter was never the binding constraint.

## RP3: re-profile after G13 — the ordering did not change, and that is the answer

The re-profile RP2's own cadence scheduled after the next landed item. Same
method, run straight off the committed `tools/hot-expert/profile_run.sh` with
only the run list changed: fresh process, tier 1296/1695/1695, 8 threads
pinned, `--greedy 128`, RP1's 42-token prompt, one frozen histogram copy,
netdata stopped, **100% residency asserted before every run** (RP2's hardened
rule), three runs per knob position, alternating, order counterbalanced the
opposite way from RP2 (RP3 starts OFF, RP2 started ON).

| bucket | RP2 off | **RP3 off** | RP2 on | **RP3 on** |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **MoE FFN total** | 67.84 | **64.98** | 67.66 | **64.68** |
| — CPU int4 experts | 38.27 | 38.76 | 38.28 | 38.59 |
| — router | 5.71 | 5.86 | 5.76 | 5.73 |
| — **shared expert (G13)** | 14.27 | **10.84** | 14.21 | **11.02** |
| — GPU expert groups | 8.45 | 8.47 | 8.41 | 8.43 |
| — bind + dispatch + accumulate | 1.15 | 1.21 | 0.98 | 0.97 |
| **KDA (34 layers)** | 53.35 | 53.43 | 31.62 | 31.66 |
| **MLA / DSA (11 layers)** | 23.67 | 23.62 | 23.85 | 24.12 |
| **mHC + RMSNorm (90 sites)** | 9.03 | 8.84 | 8.89 | 9.00 |
| dense FFN | 2.90 | 2.89 | 2.86 | 2.86 |
| lm_head | 2.61 | 2.62 | 2.59 | 2.60 |
| **total** | **159.08** | **156.77** | **137.19** | **134.90** |

Medians of three. Accounting closes to **+0.39** ms/token knob-off and
**−0.03** knob-on. **Routing bit-identical for the third campaign running** —
`experts=34099, cpu n=8909` (off) and `34098 / 8910` (on), the same figures
RP1-CORRECTION and RP2 recorded. The heat ranking is fully converged; that
confound is gone for good unless the histogram is deliberately changed.

**Residency reproduces to four decimals across a changed binary.** Every one
of the six runs left the page cache at **91.6347%, 46 shards short** — the
identical figure RP2 measured on different code, on a different day. The
mechanism is a property of the engine's allocation footprint, not of any
particular build.

### G13 confirmed from outside its own campaign — and two thirds of it arrived

The shared-expert bucket moved **14.27 → 10.84 (−3.43)** knob-off and
**14.21 → 11.02 (−3.19)** knob-on, against G13's own paired measurement of
**−3.4 ms/token**. That is the fourth item this method has now confirmed
independently of the campaign that produced it (after G11, G12, and RP2's
own pair).

**But the totals moved −2.31 and −2.29, not −3.4.** About **1.0 ms/token of
the win did not reach the bottom line**, and most of it is one bucket:

| | RP2 range | RP3 range | overlap? |
|---|---|---|---|
| CPU int4 experts, knob-off | 38.20 – 38.35 | 38.59 – 38.91 | **no**, gap +0.24 |
| CPU int4 experts, knob-on | 38.20 – 38.41 | 38.53 – 38.77 | **no**, gap +0.12 |

The CPU expert bucket is up ~1% in *both* knob positions, and the
within-campaign ranges do not overlap. **This is flagged, not explained.**
Against it: 1% is comfortably inside the ~3% cross-campaign reproducibility
this record has established (§RP2), and within-campaign spread systematically
understates cross-campaign variance — different day, different thermal
history, different allocation history, even at asserted-100% residency. For
it: it appears in both configurations, in the same direction, in the bucket
least expected to drift, and G13 did change the per-layer GPU submit pattern
that the CPU expert path overlaps with under G9. **No mechanism is
established and none is claimed.** The honest reading is that G13 delivered
its bucket in full and the token gained about two thirds of that, with the
remainder unresolved at this method's floor. Worth re-checking at RP4 rather
than theorising now: if the same offset reappears against a *different*
subsequent change, it is real and it is not G13's.

### The ranking is unchanged — in both knob positions

| knob OFF (156.77) | ms | | knob ON (134.90) | ms |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| 1. KDA | 53.43 | | 1. CPU int4 experts | 38.59 |
| 2. CPU int4 experts | 38.76 | | 2. KDA | 31.66 |
| 3. MLA / DSA | 23.62 | | 3. MLA / DSA | 24.12 |
| 4. shared expert | 10.84 | | 4. shared expert | 11.02 |
| 5. mHC + RMSNorm | 8.84 | | 5. mHC + RMSNorm | 9.00 |
| 6. GPU expert groups | 8.47 | | 6. GPU expert groups | 8.43 |
| 7. router | 5.86 | | 7. router | 5.73 |

Identical order to RP2, position for position. **G13 shrank #4 without
reordering anything**, and the two leaders are the same two with the same two
reasons they are not attackable: knob-off KDA is *already solved* by G12 and
merely not switched on; knob-on the CPU experts are VRAM-bound, not
tier-bound, and were examined and declined twice (G11 part 2, §SPEC-PROBE).

**A re-profile that reports "no change" has still earned its cost** — that is
what RP1's own lesson says, and it is the first time this track has run one
and had nothing to reorder.

### What RP3 actually says: the track has run out of measurable items

MLA is #3 in both positions and is the largest bucket with an unexploited
structure. Its split at ctx 106 is `proj` 0.62, `index` 0.205, `attn` 1.36
ms/call — the attention core is ~14.9 ms/token of the 24, and G8 already took
2.43× out of it. Everything below MLA is **≤ 11 ms/token**.

That bounds every remaining item on this track. A *very good* result against
any sub-MLA bucket — 30%, the order of what G7, G10 and G13 achieved — is
worth **≈ 3 ms/token, about 2% of the token.** The serving gate's
demonstrated noise floor is 3–4% (§G13: the pristine binary alone swung 3.7%
run-to-run; §G11 part 1: 1% was inside G10's spread). **Every individually
remaining item on the GLM track is now worth less than the serving gate can
resolve.** G13 was the last one big enough to be worth a gate run at all, and
even it came back inconclusive.

This is a structural statement about where the track is, not a counsel of
despair, and it leaves three honest options:

1. **Accept `[OPTIME]`-only validation** for further small items, with the
   serving gate demoted to a regression check rather than a measure of the
   win. Defensible — the timers hold to ~1% where tok/s scatters 3–4% — but
   it means shipping changes whose end-to-end effect is asserted, not shown.
2. **Attack a blocked leader**, which for both of them means changing VRAM
   capacity, not code. §SPEC-PROBE's reversal condition points the same way.
   A fourth card, or expert-density work, would unblock the CPU expert bucket
   (28.6% knob-on) *and* make speculative decoding viable in one move.
3. **Switch tracks.** Qwen3.8 is untouched, has never had a G3-equivalent
   profile, and Q0/Q5 are a day between them.

The roadmap should say which, rather than letting the next session pick the
largest remaining number and re-derive all of this.

## G14-PROBE: the CPU expert kernel is 2x off, and RP3's "no cheap move" was wrong

**RP3 concluded the two leading buckets were both blocked and recommended a
VRAM capacity change. That recommendation was wrong, and it was wrong in the
worst possible direction: it proposed buying hardware to escape the one
problem this engine exists to solve.** Colibri's premise is that a large MoE
lives in RAM with only the hot experts in VRAM. "The CPU expert path is
VRAM-bound" is not a terminus, it is the problem statement.

**What had actually been declined, versus what RP3 claimed.** RP3 said the
bucket was "examined and declined twice". It was not. §G11 part 2 tested three
*float-domain* variants of the existing algorithm — a bit-trick nibble→float
decode, two accumulators, four accumulators — and §SPEC-PROBE declined
speculative decoding, which is not about this path at all. **No one had tried a
different algorithm.**

**And §G3 has been pointing at one since the first profile**, in its own words:
`matmul_i4_grouped` is *"ALU/decode-bound at 27% of bandwidth"*, 19 GB/s against
this box's measured 70.9 GB/s. At RP3's 38.76 ms/token for 985 MB it is ~25
GB/s — **36% of ceiling**. The bucket has never been bandwidth-bound. It is
bound by how expensively nibbles become floats: per 16 weights the inner loop
spends two `cvtepu8_epi32`, two `sub_epi32` and two `cvtepi32_ps` to feed two
FMAs.

### The untried algorithm: integer-domain accumulation

Quantise the activation to int8 **once per matmul, per group** — O(I) against
the matmul's O(I·O), 0.05% at GLM's shapes — then run the inner loop as
`maddubs_epi16` + `madd_epi16` into int32 and apply the group scale once at the
end. Every convert leaves the inner loop. Nibbles stay unsigned 0..15 because
`maddubs` wants u8×s8; the stored value is n−8 and
`sum((n-8)·q) = sum(n·q) − 8·sum(q)`, so the bias comes off with a per-group sum
of the quantised activation computed in the same pass. No saturation is
possible: |n·q| ≤ 1920 and `maddubs` sums two, so |·| ≤ 3840, inside int16.

Measured with `tools/hot-expert/i4_kernel_bench.c` (G11's harness, sized to
defeat the 128 MB L3 — 86 matrices, 406 MB, cycled), 8 threads pinned, rig idle:

| variant | gate/up (4096×2048) | down (2048×4096) | numerics vs in-tree |
|---|---|---|---|
| baseline (in-tree) | 1.00× (17.2–21.4 GB/s) | 1.00× (13.4) | — |
| A bit-trick decode | 1.01–1.40× | 1.19× | **bit-exact** |
| B +2 accumulators | 1.07–1.64× | 1.28× | relL2 1.6e−7 |
| C +4 acc, 16B load | 1.26–1.65× | 1.44× | relL2 1.6e−7 |
| **D integer-domain** | **1.69–2.31×** (32.6–39.8 GB/s) | **2.23×** (29.8) | **relL2 3.9e−3, cos 0.999993** |

D lands at **42–56% of the DRAM ceiling** where the in-tree kernel sits at
19–30%. Three runs and two shapes; the *baseline itself* varies 17.2–21.4 GB/s
run to run, which is the same DRAM-placement bimodality §RP3 recorded for the
KDA state, so the ratios are quoted as ranges rather than a single figure.

### Sizing, and the numerics bill

At 2.0× on the kernel the bucket goes **38.7 → ~19.4 ms/token**; at a
conservative 1.7× on the whole path (the `[PROF] cpu` timer covers three
matmuls plus the swiglu, already OMP-fused by G11 part 1) it is
**38.7 → ~22.8, i.e. −16 ms/token**. Against RP3's totals that is **10% of the
knob-off token and 12% of knob-on** — three to four times the serving gate's
3–4% noise floor. **That projection did not survive the engine** — see the four-mode table
below: the isolated benchmark over-predicted by ~1.8x and the only
text-preserving mode delivers 1.05x.

The bill is a real numerics change: **relL2 3.9e−3 per matmul, cosine
0.999993**. That is four orders of magnitude coarser than the float-domain
variants and it compounds over 42 layers × 8 experts. It ships behind an env
knob, off by default, with the end-to-end logit diff in the commit body, per
CLAUDE.md — exactly the G12 treatment. **Whether it can ever be defaulted on is
an open question this probe does not answer**; the greedy-identity and
1260-position `teacher_forcing` oracles decide that, and they may well say no.

`matmul_i4_grouped` is on the **qwen38 path too**, so per §G10's lesson the new
kernel is a separate function and the existing one is not touched.

### What this says about RP3's conclusion

RP3's arithmetic — everything below MLA is ≤ 11 ms/token, so nothing left is
gate-resolvable — was correct *about the buckets it looked at* and wrong about
the two it had written off. The CPU expert bucket is 38.6 and has ~16 ms/token
of headroom sitting in an algorithm nobody had tried. **The lesson is narrower
than "re-profile more often": a bucket is only "blocked" if what was declined
is the same kind of thing as what is being proposed.** RP3 inherited "declined
twice" from two entries that had declined something else.

### Built, measured end to end, and it FAILS the numerics oracle

The kernel was implemented (`coli_i4_row_i8` + `coli_i4_quant_act` in
`quant.h`, wired into `mlp3_cpu` behind `GLM53_I4_INT8`, one activation
quantisation shared by gate and up) and put through the full oracle.

**Knob OFF is bit-identical to pristine on both prompts** — the default path
did not move, which was the first thing to establish and the only part of this
that is unambiguously good.

**Knob ON fails.** The greedy text *changes* — not a rounding wobble, a
materially different continuation ("Include SQL examples and discuss
trade-offs" → "Include the trade-offs of each strategy"):

| | short prompt | long prompt (1232 positions) |
|---|---|---|
| `teacher_forcing` cos | 1.000000000 | **0.995006** |
| `last_logits` cos | 0.997668 | **0.989639** |
| `last_logits` relL2 | 0.070 | **0.144** |
| argmax | same | same |
| **greedy text, 128 tokens** | **DIFFERS** | — |

Compare G12, which shipped opt-in at cosine 0.99992 with **identical** greedy
text. This is two orders of magnitude coarser and it changes what the model
says. It is not shippable as a default and it is a poor bargain even as a knob.

**In-engine speed is also below what the microbenchmark promised.** Paired,
`[PROF] cpu` over 128 tokens: **11.66 → 8.52 s**. But the two runs routed
differently (n=19706 vs 19055) *because the output diverged*, so the honest
figure is per-expert: **0.592 → 0.447 ms, 1.32×** — against 1.7–2.3× isolated.
The gap is the swiglu and the new quantisation pass, which the bench excluded,
plus real mmap streaming rather than a cycling array. At 1.32× the bucket
would go 38.6 → ~29, **−9 ms/token, not the −16 this probe projected.**

### All four variants, measured in the engine — and the real constraint

Four modes were built and each put through the full oracle against a pristine
binary. `GLM53_I4_INT8` selects: 1 = int8 everywhere, 2 = int16 everywhere,
3 = int8 gate/up with the down projection left on floats, 4 = float domain with
bit-trick decode and four accumulators. **Knob OFF is bit-identical to pristine
on both prompts in every build.**

| mode | inner loop | isolated | **in engine** | long TF | greedy text | verdict |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 int8 | `maddubs` | 1.7–2.3× | **1.32×** | cos 0.995 | **differs** | rejected |
| 2 int16 | `madd_epi16` | — | **1.075×** | **exact** | **differs** | rejected |
| 3 int8 g/u, float down | mixed | — | — | cos 0.998 | **differs** | rejected |
| 4 float, bit-trick + 4 acc | `fmadd` | 1.26–1.65× | **1.095×** | **exact** | **IDENTICAL** | **shipped** (`GLM53_I4_FAST`) |

Mode 4's timing pair is the only properly controlled one: `[PROF] cpu`
**11.777 → 11.215 s with n identical at 19706**, because the text is identical
so the routing is identical. The int8 and int16 pairs routed differently
(19706 vs 19055 / 18546) *because their output diverged*, so their ratios are
per-expert and weaker.

Mode 4's numerics are reassociation-only: short-prompt `last_logits` relL2
**3.0e−6** (cos 1.000000000), long-prompt `teacher_forcing` **exact over 1232
positions**, greedy text identical over 128 tokens. Its long-prompt
`last_logits` relL2 of 0.055 is real, not noise — **a pristine-vs-pristine
control on the same prompt returns exactly 0** — and is simply float
reassociation accumulating through 1232 positions of KV cache. Every prediction
still matches.

**§G11 was right and this probe's projection was wrong.** G11 declined this
kernel at "~1.06× once the path is fused"; measured in the engine it is 1.050×.
The G14-PROBE estimate of −16 ms/token came from extrapolating an isolated
microbenchmark, and that extrapolation does not hold.

### The three rejected modes were removed, and that was worth 4%

They were kept at first "as the measured record", which was sunk-cost
reasoning: the record is this file and the commit bodies, which is exactly
where CLAUDE.md says the evidence lives ("the history is the benchmark
archive"). Keeping them cost four real things — 64 KB of stack arrays in
`mlp3_cpu` that the surviving mode never touches, on a function called ~70
times per token; ~150 lines of dead `static inline` in `quant.h`, a header
qwen38 also includes, against §G10's lesson about perturbing hot shared files;
a knob whose setting 1 is genuinely 32% faster and silently degrades output,
which is a trap for anyone who benchmarks before reading; and a name,
`GLM53_I4_INT8=4`, that selected the *float* kernel.

Removing them was not free housekeeping — **it made the surviving path measurably
faster**, which is the dead stack frame disappearing:

| | pristine | f4 on | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| with modes 1–3 present | 11.777 | 11.215 | 1.050× |
| **after removing them** | 11.761 | **10.737** | **1.095×** |

Both pairs have the expert count identical at 19706. Knob OFF remains
bit-identical, knob ON remains greedy-identical, and qwen38 rebuilds clean off
the shared header. The knob is now `GLM53_I4_FAST`.

### The constraint is bytes, not ALU — which corrects §G3 for the in-engine case

The isolated-to-in-engine attenuation is large and consistent: **2.3× → 1.32×
for int8, 1.45× → 1.05× for the float kernel, roughly 0.55–0.65× either way.**
The reason is what the two settings actually stream. The microbenchmark cycles
406 MB with partial cache hits; the engine pulls **985 MB/token of cold expert
weights out of mmap'd pages**, a different expert almost every time.

So §G3's "ALU/decode-bound at 27% of bandwidth" is right *about the kernel in
isolation* and misleading *about the kernel in the engine*. **In situ the CPU
expert path is much closer to memory-bound than the isolated number implies**,
which is why making the arithmetic 2.3× faster buys 1.32×, and why making it
1.45× faster buys 1.05×.

**That redirects the whole line of attack, and it points somewhere squarely
in-premise.** If the bucket is bound by streaming cold expert bytes, the lever
is *fewer bytes*, not faster arithmetic:

- **int3 experts** (`fmt=5`, already implemented in `upload_tensor`,
  `scale_floats` and the fused gate_up shader) cut an expert from 13.5 to
  10.5 MiB — **22% fewer bytes streamed per CPU expert**, directly against the
  measured constraint.
- The *same* change fits **~28% more experts in the same 72 GB**, so fewer
  activations reach the CPU path at all.

Both effects push the same way, and neither needs a card. That is the item this
probe actually found, and it is a better one than the kernel it set out to
build. It is a bigger job — converter, a CPU-side int3 kernel, and numerics
validation on the majority of the compute, since int3 weights are a real
precision drop unlike mode 4's reassociation — and it should be sized with an
in-engine probe, not an isolated one.

### The methodological finding, which is the more useful half

**The microbenchmark's numerics figure was meaningless and its speed figure was
not.** It reported relL2 3.9e−3; the engine showed 0.070–0.144 on logits, ~30×
worse. The bench filled its matrices and activations with **uniform random
data**. Real GLM activations are not uniform — they have the outliers that
`swiglu_limit = 10.0` exists to clamp, and one outlier in a group of 64 crushes
the resolution of the other 63.

So: a microbenchmark can size *throughput* on synthetic data, because
throughput depends on instruction mix and memory traffic, which synthetic data
reproduces faithfully. **It cannot size *accuracy* on synthetic data, because
accuracy depends on the distribution, which synthetic data destroys.** §SPEC-
PROBE's cheap-probe-first pattern is still right; this is a limit on which
questions a probe can answer, and it should have been obvious before spending
the run.

### Where this leaves the item — not dead, and re-scoped

The direction is validated: **there is 30%+ sitting in this kernel and §G3's
decode-bound diagnosis was right.** The blocker is now specific and named — the
precision of the activation quantisation, not the algorithm.

The next attempt is **int16 activations via `madd_epi16`** instead of int8 via
`maddubs_epi16`: sign-extend the nibbles to int16, keep the activation at
int16, accumulate int32. Still no float convert in the inner loop, so most of
the structural win survives, but 16 lanes per register instead of 32 — expect
~1.15–1.25× rather than 1.32×, for numerics that should be near-lossless
against f32 activations at these magnitudes. A cheaper variant worth testing in
the same run: keep int8 but quantise the activation on **sub-groups of 16**
within each 64-weight group (four int32 partials, four scales), which costs
little and directly attacks the outlier problem.

**Neither can be sized on synthetic data.** The next probe measures speed in the
microbenchmark and accuracy *in the engine*, on real activations, or it does not
measure accuracy at all.

The knob ships **off by default and is not recommended at any setting** — it is
kept as the measured baseline the int16 follow-up is compared against, not as a
usable configuration. That is stated in the code.

The second in-premise lever, unexamined and recorded here so it is not lost:
**int3 experts on the GPU tier.** `fmt=5` (int3-g64, 24B per 64-group) is
already implemented in `upload_tensor`, `scale_floats` and the fused gate_up
shader. An expert slot goes 13.5 → 10.5 MiB, ~28% more experts resident in the
same 72 GB, which cuts the CPU expert count directly rather than making it
faster. Smaller and with diminishing returns (the marginal experts are the cold
ones) and a much bigger job — converter, a CPU-side int3 kernel, and numerics
validation on the majority of the compute. Second priority, but it is a second
answer that does not need a card.

## G15 (item 4h): GLM-5.3 does NOT survive int3 experts — the probe kills it

**Verdict: item 4h is dead, and it died at step 1 as the item said it would.**
No converter was written, no shader was touched, no `fmt=5` CPU kernel was
built, and **no speed was measured** — the item is explicit that speed does not
matter if accuracy fails, and it fails.

### What was run

`fmt=5` was simulated on the int4-g64 weights already on disk rather than
converted, exactly as item 4h specifies. Per 64-input subgroup of an int4 row:
`v = nibble − 8`, `amax = sc·max|v|`, `s3 = max(amax/3, 1e−8)` — a **fresh**
group scale, not the int4 one — `q = clip(rint(w/s3), −4, 3)`, `w' = q·s3`.
That is `quant_int3_g64()` in the converter and `pack_int3_g64()` in `quant.h`,
element for element. Behind `GLM53_I3_SIM=1`, paired with `GLM53_EXPERTS_CPU=1`
which forces every routed expert onto the CPU so the ~79 % the GPU tier serves
from unmodified int4 cannot mask it. Both off by default; knobs off is
bit-identical to pristine (below). Routed experts only — the shared expert and
the dense layers are untouched, which is the scope item 4h describes.

**The simulation is really int3, and that was proved before the engine ran**
(`tools/hot-expert/rome_i3sim.c`, AVX2, 8 threads):

| check | result |
|---|---|
| A the nibble→level map, exhaustive over 16 nibbles × 8 group absmaxes × 4 scales | 316 reachable cases, **0 disagreements** with `pack_int3_g64` |
| B `i4_sub_absmax` vector arm vs scalar arm, 20 000 randomised cases | **0 disagreements** |
| **C dequantise int4 → real `pack_int3_g64` → real `matmul_i3`, vs `matmul_i4_sim3`** | gate/up **relL2 1.66e−7**, down **1.44e−7**, gs=128 **1.75e−7**, cos 1.000000000 |
| D `coli_i4_rows4_sim3` vs `coli_i4_row_sim3` (the prefill path) | **0 of 800 rows** differ, bit-identical |
| F cost of the probe's double quantisation (fp8→int4→int3 vs fp8→int3) | **1.08×** on all three weight distributions |

C is the one that matters: the simulation and the tree's own shipping int3 path
differ only by the order the group partials are summed. F prices the probe's one
structural deviation — it is a double quantisation where a converter would go
fp8→int3 once — at **8 % more RMS error**, so the probe is pessimistic by 8 %
and nothing like enough to explain what follows. F also prices int3 itself:
on Gaussian weights the dequantised error is **relL2 0.246 against int4's
0.105**, i.e. **2.3× coarser weights**.

### The engine, five configurations, one prompt each

Standard 3-GPU environment, tier **1296 / 1695 / 1695** asserted up in every
run, 8 threads pinned, frozen histogram copy per run, `GLM53_PREFIX_CKPT=0` with
a private `COLI_CKPT_DIR`, GLM 89.3 % page-cache resident. Pristine = `c4a3e68`,
rebuilt from source; **its sha256 matched the binary that was in service**, so
the reference is the shipped engine and not a lookalike. Short prompt = §G1's 43
tokens (`--greedy 128 --logits`), long prompt = the same ×30, 1232 positions
(`--greedy 0 --logits`).

| pair | what changes | short: TF | short: `last_logits` cos / relL2 | long: TF | long: cos / relL2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| cand knobs off vs pristine | nothing | **identical 42/42** | **1.000000000 / 0** | — | — |
| `EXPERTS_CPU=2` vs pristine | placement only | **identical 42/42** | 0.999999996 / 9.1e−5 | **identical 1232/1232** | 0.999024 / 0.047 |
| `EXPERTS_CPU=1` vs `=2` | the swiglu clamp | 6 of 42 differ | 0.992324 / 0.124 | 8 of 1232 differ | 0.981143 / 0.199 |
| **`I3_SIM=1` vs `EXPERTS_CPU=1`** | **int3, nothing else** | **13 of 42 differ** | **0.972634 / 0.237** | **16 of 1232 differ** | **0.877795 / 0.580** |
| `I3_SIM=1` vs `EXPERTS_CPU=2` | int3, unclamped both sides | — | — | 15 of 1232 differ | 0.900644 / 0.482 |
| `I3_SIM=1` vs pristine | int3 end to end | 13 of 42 differ | 0.968277 / 0.253 | 15 of 1232 differ | 0.899303 / 0.493 |

Greedy text over 128 tokens differs for every int3 row. The last-token argmax
holds on the short prompt and **changes on the long one** (518 vs 16).

### Why this is a kill and not a tuning problem

Set against this track's own two precedents:

| | greedy text | long-prompt TF | `last_logits` cos | outcome |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| §G12 `COLI_KDA_GPU=2` | **identical** | 0 of 1260 differ | 0.99992 | shipped, opt-in |
| §G14 int8 activations | differs | cos 0.995 | 0.98964 | **rejected** |
| **G15 int3 experts** | **differs** | **16 of 1232 differ** | **0.87780** | **rejected** |

int3 is an order of magnitude past the thing this track already rejected, on the
same oracles, on the same prompts. The gate item 4h wrote — greedy identity plus
`teacher_forcing` — is failed on both, at both lengths, and the failure is
inside the arithmetic rather than at the margin: 3-bit absmax over 64 weights is
simply not enough resolution for this model's experts. It cannot be recovered by
a better kernel, because there is no kernel involved; it is the format.

**The 8 % double-quantisation pessimism does not save it.** Removing it entirely
would move the long-prompt cosine from 0.8778 to roughly 0.89 — still five times
further from 1 than the rejected G14 kernel.

### The thing the probe found on the way, which is worth more than the verdict

**The routed-expert GPU kernel does not apply GLM-5.3's swiglu clamp, and that
is now measured rather than noted.** §G13 found the gap (`qmatmul_gate_up.comp`
computes `silu(gate)*up` with no `limit` push constant, while `mlp3_cpu` calls
`swiglu_clamped` at `swiglu_limit = 10.0`) and left it as a finding because
nothing had ever put a clamped and an unclamped computation of the same op side
by side. This probe had to, because its control run did not behave:

- placement alone (`EXPERTS_CPU=2`, CPU path with the clamp disabled so it
  computes what the GPU computes) is **`teacher_forcing`-identical over 42 and
  over 1232 positions**, cosine 0.999999996 / 0.999024. The two expert paths are
  the same function up to float reassociation. The knob does only what it says.
- turning the clamp on for the ~79 % of experts the GPU serves changes **6 of 42
  and 8 of 1232 teacher-forced predictions**, cosine 0.9923 / 0.9811, **and it
  changes the final argmax on the long prompt**. On the short prompt it also
  sends the greedy continuation into a degenerate `Searched the web for …` loop
  where the pristine answers the question.

So GLM-5.3's output today depends on **which experts happen to be tier-resident**
— an expert served from VRAM and the same expert served from RAM compute
different functions whenever `|gate|` or `|up|` exceeds 10.0. That is not a
rounding difference and it is not what `swiglu_limit` is in the config for. It
is a bigger effect than several of the wins this track has landed. **It is not
in scope for G15 and it is not fixed here**; it is recorded as the next real
item on this engine, and `GLM53_EXPERTS_CPU=1/2` is the instrument that measures
it. **The knobs stay in the tree for that reason** (CLAUDE.md: do not remove a
knob a recorded measurement depends on); neither is a trap in §G14's sense,
because both are strictly *slower* than the default, so nobody benchmarking will
turn one on by accident.

### Two methodological notes

**The greedy-text oracle alone would have been ambiguous here, and the
`teacher_forcing` oracle was not.** On this prompt the model is chaotic: pure
float reassociation (control B, cosine 0.999999996) already changes the 128-token
continuation at character 370, and the *int3* run produced a visibly *better*
continuation than the int4 control did. Text quality read by eye would have
given the wrong answer in both directions. `teacher_forcing` — 42 and 1232
independent teacher-forced predictions — is what separates 0 differences from
16, and it is why CLAUDE.md names it as the oracle for this engine.

**§G14's rule held again, in the other direction.** A synthetic microbenchmark
cannot size accuracy: the harness said int3 costs relL2 0.169 per matmul on
uniform data and the engine showed relL2 0.58 on the logits. What a synthetic
harness *can* size is a ratio between two quantisers (test F, 1.08× on three
distributions) and whether one implementation equals another (test C, 1.7e−7),
and both of those carried real weight here.

### Reproducing it

`tools/hot-expert/g15_probe.sh [short|clamp|long|all]` on the rig (takes the rig
lock, stops and restores the gateway, restores the in-service binary on every
exit path), `tools/hot-expert/g15_compare.py` for the three oracles,
`tools/hot-expert/rome_i3sim.c` for the transform oracle. Logs:
`~/bench/g15_probe.log`, `~/bench/g15_probe2.log`, outputs in
`~/bench/g15_probe_out/`. Binaries: `~/bench/glm53.g15pristine` (= the binary in
service) and `~/bench/glm53.g15cand`.

## C2 / phase-Q baseline: qwen38-vk's four steady-state numbers

Track G is finalised and merged (`2c311a0`), so the rig was switched to Qwen3.8
and given the same four numbers §G0 established for GLM. This is the line every
track-Q item will be measured against.

`tools/rome_bench.sh qwen38-vk`, cap 512, max-new 80, 8 threads pinned, netdata
stopped, **GPU tier asserted up by the harness at 14,673 experts** (not the
`| GPU |` row — the engine's own `[qwen38] Vulkan tier preloaded` line, per §C0).

| run | rotating median | warm-identical | cold |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| q-baseline-run1 | **4.06** | 5.45 | 2.32 |
| q-baseline-run2 | **4.10** | 5.46 | 2.40 |

Rotating and warm-identical reproduce to ~1% and agree with the 2026-09-04
`gpu-real1/2` rows (4.12 / 4.13 and 5.43 / 5.44) within the harness's own noise.

**The cold column does not, and it is flagged rather than recorded as a
regression: 3.47 → 2.32 / 2.40, about 31% worse.** Nothing about the model or
the binary explains that. The likely cause is what the page cache held going in:
the 09-04 rows were taken with Qwen already warm from prior activity, so their
"cold" request was not paying disk, whereas today's runs started with **GLM's
182 GiB resident** and Qwen had to displace it. On that reading today's figure
is the more honest cold number and 3.47 was optimistic — but that is a
hypothesis, not a measurement, and the way to settle it is a drop-caches run
(needs sudo, so an owner action). **Do not treat 3.47 as the cold baseline for
track Q; treat 2.3–2.4 as provisional and re-establish it deliberately.**

Note the tier scale difference before porting any GLM intuition: qwen38's tier
holds **14,673 experts** where GLM's holds 4,686. Items that were VRAM-bound on
GLM are not automatically VRAM-bound here.

### qwen38-vk after the mapping change (2026-09-09 05:52–06:11)

The upstream `dev` merge moved the expert mapping into `c/st.h`
(`st_map_shard_range`, on by default for qwen38); the merge gate compiled
`qwen38-vk` and ran its four C tests but never ran it on a model. Taken by
`~/bench/q38remap_chain.sh`: gateway stopped, GLM dropped from the page cache
with `posix_fadvise(DONTNEED)`, Qwen3.8 warmed and asserted, `rome_bench.sh
qwen38-vk` twice plus a `COLI_MAP_EXPERTS=0` A/B, Qwen dropped, GLM re-warmed
by `~/start_glm53.sh` and the gateway back at 06:11. **Correction, checked
directly after this section was first drafted:** `fincore` immediately after
the restart read GLM at only 91.72 % resident (`size=194727335256
res=178612793344`) — under the 96 % floor this procedure requires, with
64 GB of RAM sitting idle at the time (nothing forced the shortfall by
memory pressure). A second, manual re-read of all 62 shards (21 s) brought it
to 100.00 % resident with the gateway still serving (`curl /v1/models` → 200)
throughout; `~/start_glm53.sh`'s own warm-before-serve step should not be
trusted from a single post-restart `fincore` reading. GPU tier asserted at
**14 673** experts in every run (dev0 24.4/25.7 GB), cap 512, max-new 80,
8 threads.

| run | rotating | warm-identical | cold |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| q-remap-run1 (mapping on) | **4.17** | 5.25 | 3.38 |
| q-remap-run2 (mapping on) | **4.31** | 5.25 | 3.44 |
| q-remap-nomap (`COLI_MAP_EXPERTS=0`) | 4.16 | 5.24 | 3.40 |
| q-baseline-run1 (2026-09-06) | 4.06 | 5.45 | 2.32 |
| q-baseline-run2 (2026-09-06) | 4.10 | 5.46 | 2.40 |
| 2026-09-04 gpu-real1/run2 | 4.12 / 4.13 | 5.43 / 5.44 | 3.47 / 3.47 |

Rotating **+2.7 % (run1 vs 09-06 run1) / +5.1 % (run2 vs 09-06 run2)**
against the 09-06 rows, avg **+3.9 %**; warm-identical avg **−3.8 %**; cold
avg **3.41 against 2.3–2.4** provisional, close to the 09-04 rows' 3.47.
`datapoint.py`'s own `evict_cache` drops the Qwen shards with
`posix_fadvise(DONTNEED)` immediately before spawning the persistent engine
regardless of what `rome_bench.sh` pre-warmed, so the cold column is a real
disk-cold figure on every row, not an artefact of page-cache state at
measurement time; what differs between runs is what else was resident when
the reload happened. The 09-06 cold column was flagged provisional in §C2 on
the hypothesis that GLM's 182 GiB was still resident and had to be evicted
under memory pressure while Qwen reloaded; this chain dropped GLM first, so
there was clean RAM for the reload, and its cold row (3.41) lands close to
the equally-clean 09-04 rows (3.47) rather than near 09-06's 2.3–2.4 — which
supports that hypothesis without proving it (still one measurement, not a
repeat of 09-06 under controlled memory pressure). The mapping itself reads
as inert on these numbers: the `COLI_MAP_EXPERTS=0` row (4.16 / 5.24 / 3.40)
sits inside the two mapping-on rows on every column, about half the size of
run1-vs-run2's own 3.4 % spread. **Rotating and warm-identical each moved
just outside the record's own 3 % reproducibility bound (+3.9 % / −3.8 %) —
reported as a finding, not a failure, per instruction**; run1 vs run2 differ
by 3.4 % on their own (4.17 vs 4.31), on the same order as the move against
baseline and the kind of run-to-run spread already on record here (e.g. G10:
2.60 vs 2.75, 5.8 %, before any code changed), so this reads as the rig's
ordinary noise band rather than a regression signature tied to the mapping.
No change to record for the mapping; the Q-track baseline stands at
4.1–4.3 / 5.2–5.3 / 3.4 (cold now provisional-settled rather than
provisional-open).

cross-reference against the narrative sections above for interpretation.

The `engine` column names the binary, not the configuration — a `qwen38-vk`
binary with `Q38_VULKAN` unset is a CPU run. The signal that the tier is up
is the engine's own stderr line (`[qwen38] Vulkan tier preloaded N of M`,
`qwen38_core.h:1054`; `[VK] preload:`, `glm53.c:1669`), **not** datapoint.py's
`| GPU |` row: that row comes from the SERVE hwinfo frame, which qwen38 does
not send even with all three devices loaded. Rows from 2026-09-04T11:08Z on
are written only after the harness asserts those lines.

## PREFILL-PROFILE (2026-09-06) — SPEC-PROBE's data read as a prefill profile

The SPEC-PROBE runs above (same 330 tokens, `GLM53_PREFILL_CHUNK` 1→16,
`COLI_TIMERS=1`) are a prefill profile; nobody read them as one. In ms per
prompt token:

| bucket | K=1 | K=2 | K=4 | K=8 | K=16 | K1/K16 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| layers (total) | 183.6 | 175.7 | 169.9 | 167.2 | 169.8 | 1.08× |
| ffn_moe | 90.1 | 88.0 | 85.1 | 84.1 | 83.9 | 1.07× |
| — cpu experts `[PROF]` | 56.1 | 55.7 | 55.4 | 55.3 | 54.9 | 1.02× |
| — eg `[PROF]` (overlaps cpu) | 65.6 | 65.6 | 64.4 | 63.5 | 62.5 | 1.05× |
| — router | 5.7 | 5.6 | 5.6 | 5.5 | 5.5 | 1.05× |
| — shared expert | 14.3 | 15.7 | 14.4 | 14.4 | 15.2 | 0.94× |
| kda | 54.1 | 50.7 | 49.4 | 49.3 | 49.7 | 1.09× |
| — kda.proj | 22.1 | 19.8 | 19.1 | 19.0 | 19.1 | 1.16× |
| — kda.step | 13.2 | 12.6 | 12.2 | 11.7 | 11.6 | 1.14× |
| — kda.ko | 13.4 | 12.8 | 12.8 | 13.2 | 13.7 | 0.97× |
| mla | 27.5 | 25.5 | 24.1 | 22.7 | 24.4 | 1.13× |
| — mla.proj | 6.9 | 6.2 | 6.0 | 5.9 | 5.8 | 1.18× |
| — mla.attn | 17.1 | 16.9 | 16.3 | 15.1 | 17.1 | 1.00× |
| hc+norm | 9.0 | 8.7 | 8.5 | 8.4 | 8.9 | 1.01× |

`[PROF] eg experts=` fell 75 628 → 47 921 (K=1 → 16) while eg time fell 3 %:
the chunk union *does* dedupe expert calls, and the GPU expert cost is per
**row**, not per expert (`qmatmul_gate_up.comp` dispatches `(O/8, rows, 1)`,
each row re-reading the expert). `[PROF] cpu n=` (35 252 at every K) counts
(token, expert) pairs and cannot show dedup — SPEC-PROBE's "cold experts do
not dedup" was drawn from the wrong counter and is withdrawn.

Nothing batches because nothing is called batched: `kda_layer`, `mla_layer`,
the shared expert, the router and `mlp3_cpu` all loop `for t < tokens` and
issue M=1 work per token, though every kernel they call already takes `S`.
At K=1 the streaming wall (cpu experts) is 31 %, the recurrence 7 %, and 62 %
is batchable work done unbatched. Conclusion and items:
`PREFILL-ROADMAP-2026-09.md` rev 2.

## P0 / P1 (2026-09-06) — serve-path TTFT baseline, prefix reuse measured

Instrument: `tools/hot-expert/ttft_serve.py --engine` (the gateway's own
`Engine` class, SUBMIT/DATA/DONE over the pipe), pristine build
`4e39b6b0c0d34565d1185e2cc2702454` (11:21), serving env (`COLI_KDA_GPU=2`,
1695/1695, 8 cores), chunk 128, engine warm, residency 99.13 % (7 shards
short — the serving regime's number; 100 % is unreachable with an engine up,
see `ttft_serve.py`), gateway stopped, one request in flight.

| prompt tokens (engine count) | TTFT run 1 | TTFT run 2 | ms/token | prefill tok/s |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 21 | 7.36 s (first after warm-up) | 3.08 s | 147 | 6.8 |
| 384 | 66.70 s | 65.64 s | 171 | 5.8 |
| 1 230 | 241.53 s | 241.20 s | 196 | 5.1 |

Reproducible within 1 %. Decode after the first token 4.1–6.0 tok/s (8-token
sanity window). ms/token grows with length (196 at 1 230 vs 171 at 384): the
sparse attention and the per-chunk expert union both scale with position.

**P1 — prefix reuse fires on a real turn 2** (engine's own `REUSE id reused
prompt_tokens` line, `GLM53_VERBOSE=1`):

| turn | prompt tokens | reused | TTFT |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 `[user A]` (384 tokens, 24 generated) | 384 | 0 | 65.45 s |
| 2 `[A, reply, user B]` | 424 | **408** | **2.74 s** |
| 3 `[user A]` again (regenerate) | 384 | 0 | 65.76 s |

Turn 2 re-prefilled only the 16 new tokens. The rev-1 claim "prefix reuse does
not fire (184 s then 179 s)" was the *regenerate* case: `serve_turn` resumes a
slot only from the exact position it stepped to (prompt + reply; the KDA state
cannot rewind, `glm53.c` "lo stato ricorrente ... non si riavvolge"), so an
identical prompt can never reuse — by design, not a bug. The template's
`content.strip()` on the assistant history did not break the match here.

What breaks it for Open WebUI (to be confirmed from the server log, which now
carries REUSE lines): `KV_SLOTS=1` means every side request the app makes
(title, tags, follow-up suggestions) lands on the conversation's only slot and
evicts its session; and any per-turn change before the cached point (memory
context, date in the system prompt) forces a full re-prefill. Note the gateway
already routes conversations to slots by a hash of system + first user message
(`conversation_cache_slot`) when `--kv-slots > 1` — but with `COLI_KDA_GPU=2`
the device holds ONE KDA state per layer, re-seeded on every `session_open`
(`coli_vk_kda_init`), so multiple slots need the CPU recurrence or a per-slot
device state; that is P6's actual scope.

**CANCEL (G16 gate, now executable):** 1 230-token prompt, CANCEL sent at
5.0 s → engine confirmed after **251.3 s** (ran the whole prefill and the
generation); the next request then served normally. FAIL, as the roadmap says.

### P0 self-test (`prefill_gate.sh pristine pristine`, 2026-09-06 16:34–16:48)

Oracle half: `teacher_forcing: IDENTICAL (782 positions)`, `logits:
cosine=1.0000000 max_abs=0` — the comparison machinery is sound. The self-test
also caught three instrument bugs, all fixed the same hour (`f2b185d`,
`87de6e3`, `23ed436`):

1. `ttft_serve.py` imported `openai_server` from *beside the binary*; a
   pristine copy in `~/bench` crashed the TTFT half (now: the repo's `c/`).
2. Both scripts took `COLI_VK_SHADERS` from beside the binary too, so the
   pristine copy ran **without Vulkan**: 414 ms/token, zero GPU experts, zero
   GPU binds — a CPU-only number that would have passed for a baseline. Now:
   the repo's `c/shaders`, and both scripts refuse to run if it is missing.
3. The CLI profile passed no cache cap, so the engine sized its expert cache
   from MemAvailable and evicted the model under itself (100 % → 91.7 %
   during one prefill). Now: cap 512, the same the serve harness uses.

The lesson for every future instrument here: **a gate must fail loudly on a
misconfiguration, not produce a plausible number.** A 2.4 tok/s "baseline"
would have made every later item look 2× better than it was.

## P2 (2026-09-06) — batching the dense prefill stages, gated

`P2-BATCH-DENSE-SPEC-2026-09.md`; commits `b9c193e` (P2.2/P2.3), `23ed436`
(P2.1/P2.4), `396ff42`, `0b75c42`. Gate: `prefill_gate.sh` pristine
`4e39b6b0…` vs candidate, oracle at `COLI_KDA_GPU=0` cap 512, TTFT at the
serving default `COLI_KDA_GPU=2`.

**p2 (P2.2/P2.3 only, 17:25)** — TTFT 1.09× at 21/384/1 230 tokens (3.08 →
2.78 s, 65.5 → 60.1 s, 241.3 → 221.0 s); **decode sanity window 5.4 → 2.1
tok/s**. Cause: three `#pragma omp parallel for if(hc_par)` clauses — 181
one-thread libgomp regions per decoded token under `OMP_PROC_BIND=close`.
Replaced by C-level branches (`396ff42`); the batched loops now exist only
for n > 1. (The oracle half of this run was invalid: the pristine copy ran
without Vulkan — see the P0 self-test.)

**p2c (full P2, 17:29–18:16)** — oracle **PASS**: `teacher_forcing: IDENTICAL
(782 positions)`, `logits: cosine=1.0000000 max_abs=0`. Profile, 781-token
prompt, chunk 128, `COLI_KDA_GPU=0`, ms/token:

| bucket | pristine | P2 | |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| total | 206.3 | 175.3 | 1.18× |
| kda | 50.9 | 31.5 | proj 19.0→12.6, ko 14.6→5.7, decay+norm 5.4→0.7, step 11.8→12.6 |
| shared | 11.3 | 4.7 | |
| hc+norm | 8.4 | 4.6 | |
| mla | 36.2 | 33.9 | proj 6.2→4.5; attn 26.7 unchanged |
| router | 5.5 | 5.2 | scalar dot is compute-bound (9.6 GMAC/s on 8 threads) either way |
| ffn_moe | 105.7 | 100.0 | cpu 84 ‖ eg 88, untouched |
| head | 2.3 | 2.3 | the oracle keeps every row |

Serve path (`COLI_KDA_GPU=2`), decode window normal (5.5–6.2 tok/s) on both:

| tokens | pristine | P2 (p2c) | |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 21 | 3.07 s | 14.6 s | **0.21×** |
| 384 | 65.9 / 65.6 s | 101.0 / 97.9 s | 0.65× |
| 1 230 | 241.3 / 241.2 s | 354.1 / 351.2 s | 0.68× |

The oracle passed and the chain served the candidate: **the gate did not
fail on a TTFT regression by design, and the chain only checked the exit
code** — fixed the same evening (`MIN_SPEEDUP`, exit 3, decode within 10 %).
Cause of the regression: `kda_layer_rows` ran the recurrence on the CPU copy
of the state and called `coli_vk_kda_sync` / `coli_vk_kda_upload` per layer
per chunk; the sync is a `memcpy` *from* the host-visible device arena
(write-combined memory), 34 × 4 MB per chunk at tens of MB/s ≈ 11 s even on
a one-chunk prompt. `0b75c42` keeps the state on the device and runs one
`coli_vk_kda_step` per token inside the chunk (the chain's own shader and
round-trip count); the CPU path is untouched. Re-gated as p2d.

Two facts for the next items: (1) the S-row dense GPU matmul re-reads its
weights per row (`(O/8, S, 1)` dispatch), so kda.proj+ko batched still cost
18 ms/token where a tiled GEMM would cost ~2 — P4's S-tile shader pays for
the dense path as well as the experts; (2) the router's 5 ms/token is a
scalar dot GCC does not vectorise; vectorising it changes the summation
order (a knob), worth ~4 ms/token.

**p2d (P2 with the state on the device, `0b75c42`, 18:20–18:58) — gate
PASS on all three criteria.** Oracle: teacher_forcing identical (782),
logits cosine 1.0 / max_abs 0. Serve path (`COLI_KDA_GPU=2`), residency
99.13 % both sides:

| tokens | pristine | P2 | speedup | decode p/c |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 21 | 3.06 s | 2.79 s | 1.10× | 6.0 / 5.7 |
| 384 | 65.9 / 65.2 s | 63.0 / 61.8 s | 1.05× | 5.3 / 5.1 |
| 1 230 | 240.6 / 240.1 s | 227.7 / 227.6 s | 1.06× | 3.9 / 3.9 |

`tworeq`: identical across 3 requests. **Landed and in service** (binary
`32ad1f7b…`).

Why 1.06× and not the spec's ~1.5×: the 1.18× profile was taken at
`COLI_KDA_GPU=0`, where the pristine issues per-token projections through
`vk_batch_mv_chain` and runs the recurrence on the CPU; at the serving knob
the pristine's fused per-token chain keeps every intermediate on the device
and was already cheap, so batching the projections only removes the per-
token submit overhead — and the S-row dense shader still reads the weights
once per row (`(O/8, S, 1)`), so the batched proj+ko cost 18 ms/token for
~2 ms of arithmetic. The batching structure is what P4's tiled shader needs;
the gain moves there. Rule added to the roadmap: **time the profile at the
serving knob**, keep `COLI_KDA_GPU=0` for the identity check only.

## P4 (2026-09-06, 20:49–21:20) — S-tiled Vulkan matmul shaders, dense and expert, gated

`f0197d0` (`P4-STILE-SHADER-SPEC-2026-09.md`): `qmatmul_tile.comp` and
`qmatmul_gate_up_tile.comp`, one workgroup row per tile of R=8 input rows,
each lane loading a packed weight word once and applying it to R
accumulators with the per-row shaders' per-word arithmetic and the same
`subgroupAdd`; `build_pipeline_on()` puts the tiled pipelines on the existing
layouts; dispatched for S > 1 and fmt 1/4 in `coli_vk_matmul`,
`coli_vk_gate_up`, `coli_vk_matmul_pair` and the expert groups on all three
devices (per expert with more than one row). `COLI_VK_TILE=0` disables.
Gate vs the P3 binary `112e6834…`, `MIN_SPEEDUP=1.0`: **PASS on all three
criteria** — `teacher_forcing: IDENTICAL (782 positions)`, `logits:
cosine=1.0000000 max_abs=0` (**the tiled reduction is bit-identical; no knob
needed**), `tworeq` identical. Profile (`COLI_KDA_GPU=0`, 781 tokens, chunk
128): **144.9 → 132.3 ms/token**, kda 31.7 → 22.7 (the dense tile), ffn_moe
68.4 → 66.3 (at this knob the CPU half still bounds the bucket), mla 35.1 →
33.5. Serve path (`COLI_KDA_GPU=2`):

| tokens | P3 | P4 | speedup | decode p/c |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 21 | 2.62 s | 2.37 s | 1.11× | 5.6 / 5.8 |
| 384 | 51.8 / 50.9 s | 46.1 / 45.3 s | **1.13×** | 5.1 / 4.9 |
| 1 230 | 192.6 / 192.9 s | 173.1 / 173.1 s | **1.11×** | 3.9 / 3.8 |

**Landed, in service** (`ff933414…`). **Cumulative for the day** vs the
pristine `4e39b6b0…` (P2 + P3 + P4, every one bit-identical): 21 tokens
3.07 → 2.37 s (1.30×), 384 tokens 65.6 → 45.7 s (**1.44×**), 1 230 tokens
240.3 → 173.1 s (**1.39×**).

### Profile at the serving knob (`COLI_KDA_GPU=2`, 781 tokens, chunk 128, cap 512, 2026-09-06 21:2x)

| bucket | P3 (`112e6834…`) | P4 (`ff933414…`) | note |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| **total** | **136.2** | **125.5** | ms/token; 7.34 → 7.97 tok/s |
| ffn_moe | 68.2 | 66.0 | cpu experts 53.3 → 53.5 ‖ eg 57.9 → **58.0**: the tile did not move the expert group |
| — router / shared | 5.2 / 4.7 | 5.2 / 2.4 | |
